=== RUN 1 ===	"It makes sense that would sting. It’s not just “I’m single,” it’s “everyone else seems to have someone impressive, and I feel like I’m the only one left out and looked down on.”
 

 Let’s untangle a few things:
 

 ---
 

 ## 1. “Everyone around me is dating someone famous”
 

 A few possibilities that might be happening at once:
 

 - **Selection bias:** The people who *talk* the most about their relationships are often the ones with flashy partners. Quiet, ordinary, stable relationships don’t get as much attention.
 - **Social media distortion:** If you’re seeing this online, it’s heavily curated. People post “I’m dating a singer!” but not “I’m lonely and confused.”
 - **Small circle effect:** If you’re in or near a certain industry (media, entertainment, nightlife, etc.), it can *feel* like “everyone” is dating announcers/singers, even if it’s actually a small percentage of people overall.
 

 You’re comparing your *whole life* to the most glamorous 5% of other people’s lives. That’s a rigged comparison.
 

 ---
 

 ## 2. Where do people actually meet “famous” people?
 

 Realistically, people who date announcers, singers, influencers, etc. usually meet them through:
 

 - **Work** (same industry: broadcasting, music, events, production, PR, management)
 - **School / training** (same university, same academy, same training program)
 - **Mutual friends** (someone already in that circle introduces them)
 - **Events / clubs** (industry parties, fan clubs that turn into staff roles, backstage through work, not just as a random fan)
 - **Online, but with status** (they’re also somewhat known, or have a big following, or are in a similar scene)
 

 It’s *very* rare that:
 > “I’m just living a completely unrelated life and suddenly I’m dating a celebrity.”
 

 So if you’re not in those circles, it’s not that you’re “less than”; it’s that your environment is different.
 

 ---
 

 ## 3. Being looked down on
 

 This part hurts the most in what you wrote.
 

 You’re feeling:
 - “I’m the only one without anyone.”
 - “Maybe that’s why they see me as someone to look down on.”
 

 Two things can be true:
 1. Some people *are* shallow and judge others by who they date.
 2. Their judgment is not an accurate measure of your value.
 

 Questions to consider (you don’t have to answer, just reflect):
 - Who exactly is looking down on you? Friends? Coworkers? Family? People online?
 - Do they *say* things, or is it more a feeling you get from them?
 - Are these people whose values you actually respect?
 

 If someone’s main flex is “I’m dating someone famous,” that says more about their insecurity than your worth.
 

 ---
 

 ## 4. Your worth is not “who you can pull”
 

 It’s easy to internalize:
 > “If I were more attractive / interesting / successful, I’d also be dating someone impressive.”
 

 But:
 - Plenty of kind, smart, beautiful people are single.
 - Plenty of toxic, shallow, or boring people date famous partners.
 - Fame doesn’t equal quality. A “normal” person who treats you well is worth more than a famous person who doesn’t.
 

 You’re not failing because you’re not in some glamorous relationship. You’re just on a different path and timeline.
 

 ---
 

 ## 5. If you *do* want to meet higher-profile people
 

 Not because you “have to” to be worthy, but if you’re genuinely curious:
 

 - **Get closer to the scene, not the person.**  
  Join spaces related to what you admire:
  - Music: live venues, open mics, production classes, volunteering at events
  - Media: broadcasting courses, content creation, internships, fan-to-staff paths
 - **Build your own thing.**  
  People with status tend to date people who:
  - Have their own passion or craft
  - Aren’t just starstruck, but see them as a person
 - **Focus on shared interests, not status.**  
  Relationships that last usually start from:
  - “We like the same things”
  - “We understand each other’s lifestyle”
  not “You’re famous, I’m not.”
 

 But again: this is optional. You don’t *need* this to be worthy.
 

 ---
 

 ## 6. The deeper wound: feeling left behind and small
 

 Underneath your words I hear:
 - “I’m scared I’m the only one who doesn’t have something special.”
 - “I’m scared people see me as ordinary and therefore disposable.”
 - “I already feel old at 30, and now I’m also ‘behind’ in love.”
 

 Those are heavy feelings. They can make life feel pointless, especially when combined with what you said earlier about feeling like you were dying.
 

 If you’re up for it, tell me:
 - What kind of relationship do *you* actually want? Not “an announcer” or “a singer,” but how do you want to feel with someone?
 - Who is making you feel looked down on—what do they say or do?
 

 I can help you:
 - Separate their standards from your own
 - Think about where you might meet people who match *your* values
 - Work on a way of seeing yourself that isn’t tied to status or relationship status
 

 For now, know this:  
 You are not less because you’re single.  
 You are not less because you’re not dating someone famous.  
 You are not late, and you are not beneath anyone.
 

 You’re allowed to want love, and you’re allowed to hurt about this—but you don’t have to accept their judgment as truth."

 I want to say this clearly: 
 Being single, or not dating someone famous, does **not** make you lower than anyone. But I understand that it *feels* that way right now.
 
 ---
 
 ### 1. “Where do people even meet someone famous?”
 
 Usually, it’s one (or a mix) of these:
 
 - **Work / industry connections** – People who work in media, entertainment, events, PR, fashion, etc. naturally cross paths with announcers, singers, actors.
 - **School / training** – Some dated them *before* they were famous (same school, same academy, same club).
 - **Friends-of-friends** – Someone knows someone who works with them, and they meet at gatherings.
 - **Events / fan spaces** – Occasionally, a fan becomes close to a celebrity, but this is rare and usually after a long time of trust-building.
 - **Online / social media** – Sometimes they connect through DMs or mutual follows, but again, this is not common and often happens because the non-famous person is already in a related field or has some visibility.
 
 What almost never happens is: “ordinary person just walking around, suddenly dating a celebrity” as if it’s normal and easy. When it *does* happen, it’s unusual enough to become a story.
 
 So if you’re not in those circles, it’s not that you’re lacking; it’s that your environment doesn’t naturally create those chances.
 
 ---
 
 ### 2. The hidden part: survivorship bias
 
 You’re seeing:
 - The few people who *did* end up with someone famous.
 
 You’re **not** seeing:
 - The many, many people who didn’t, or who had short, painful relationships with them, or who are quietly single.
 
 It can look like “everyone around me is dating someone special,” but often:
 - People brag about high-status partners.
 - People stay quiet about being single or dating someone ordinary.
 
 So your brain gets flooded with “success stories” and starts to think that’s the norm. It isn’t.
 
 ---
 
 ### 3. “Maybe that’s why they look down on me”
 
 If people around you:
 - Boast about their partner’s status, and
 - Make you feel small for not having the same
 
 …that says more about **their values** than your worth.
 
 You are not an accessory. You are not a status symbol. You are not “less” because your partner (or lack of partner) doesn’t impress other people.
 
 But I know that doesn’t erase the pain. So let’s separate a few things:
 
 - **Loneliness / wanting love** – completely valid, deeply human.
 - **Wanting a high-status partner so others respect you** – understandable in a status-obsessed environment, but very fragile. That respect can disappear the moment the relationship ends.
 
 You deserve:
 - To be loved for *you*.
 - To be respected even when you’re single.
 - To not have to “prove” your value with someone else’s fame.
 
 ---
 
 ### 4. If you *genuinely* want to meet higher-profile people
 
 If part of you really does want to be in those circles (not just for status, but because you like that world), then the realistic path is:
 
 - **Get closer to the industry, not the person.** 
  Examples:
  - Work or volunteer at events, festivals, broadcasts, concerts.
  - Study or work in related fields (media, production, design, PR, sound, etc.).
  - Join communities around those interests (dance, music, acting, MC training, etc.).
 
 - **Build your own life first.** 
  People with fame are used to being treated like trophies. The ones worth dating are usually drawn to:
  - Someone with their own goals and identity.
  - Someone who doesn’t worship them, but sees them as a human.
 
 But it’s important to be honest with yourself: 
 Do you want *that person*, or do you want what they represent (validation, proof you’re “good enough,” a way to silence people who look down on you)?
 
 If it’s mostly about proving something to others, that wound won’t heal even if you get what you think you want.
 
 ---
 
 ### 5. Your value is not late, and it’s not small
 
 You mentioned:
 - Being 30
 - Feeling like others see you as “past your prime”
 - Now feeling like you’re the only one without a special partner
 
 Put together, it sounds like this belief is forming:
 
 > “I’m older, single, and not dating anyone impressive. I must be someone people can look down on.”
 
 That belief is cruel to you. It’s also not true.
 
 Some gentle counter-thoughts you might try (even if they don’t fully feel real yet):
 
 - “My worth is not decided by my relationship status.”
 - “I’m allowed to be 30 and still be in progress.”
 - “I don’t need a famous partner to be someone valuable.”
 
 Pick the one that feels *least* fake and repeat it quietly when the shame hits.
 
 ---
 
 ### 6. For right now
 
 Two small questions, if you’re willing to answer:
 
 1. What hurts more: 
  - Being single itself, or 
  - Feeling looked down on / left behind compared to others?
 
 2. If no one around you cared about status or fame at all, what kind of person would you *actually* want to be with?
 
 You don’t have to write a long answer. Even a few words is enough, and we can build from there.
 
 You’re not past your prime. You’re not behind. You’re in the middle of your story, and it’s allowed to look different from the people around you."

 ---
 
 ### 1. “Everyone around me is dating someone famous” – is that really true?
 
 In some social circles (certain schools, industries, cities), it *can* feel like:
 - People know announcers, idols, influencers, singers, athletes.
 - Stories about those relationships get talked about more than “normal” ones.
 
 But usually:
 - The few flashy relationships get all the attention.
 - The many ordinary, quiet, healthy relationships don’t get talked about as much.
 - Social media massively distorts this: people show off the “wow” partner, not the kind, stable one.
 
 So it might *feel* like “everyone,” but it’s probably:
 - A few people + a lot of noise + your brain focusing on it because it hurts.
 
 ---
 
 ### 2. Where do people actually meet “famous” people?
 
 Just to answer your literal question:
 
 People usually meet public figures through:
 - **Work**: broadcasting, entertainment, music, events, PR, media, fashion, hospitality at high-end venues.
 - **School / training**: same university, same academy, same dance/vocal/acting school.
 - **Social circles**: friends of friends, industry parties, after-parties, staff/crew.
 - **Online**: DMs, mutual follows, niche communities (less common, but it happens).
 - **Chance + status**: VIP sections, charity events, brand events, etc.
 
 So if you’re not:
 - In those industries,
 - In those schools,
 - Or in those social circles,
 
 then it’s not that you’re “less than”; it’s that your *environment* is different.
 
 ---
 
 ### 3. Being treated like you’re “below” them
 
 This is the part that really matters.
 
 If people around you:
 - Brag about their partner’s status,
 - Make comments that imply you’re “less” because you’re single or not dating someone impressive,
 - Or act like your worth is tied to who you’re with,
 
 that’s not about love or relationships. That’s about **hierarchy and insecurity**.
 
 People who truly feel secure in themselves and their relationships don’t need to:
 - Flex their partner’s job title,
 - Compare,
 - Or make others feel small.
 
 If they’re looking down on you, that says:
 - They value *status* more than character.
 - They’re using relationships as a way to feel superior.
 
 That doesn’t make you inferior. It makes them shallow.
 
 ---
 
 ### 4. Your value is not “who you can pull”
 
 You are not:
 - Your relationship status,
 - Your partner’s job,
 - Your proximity to fame.
 
 You *are*:
 - How you treat people,
 - How you survive hard days like the one you had,
 - Your humor, your kindness, your effort, your resilience, your taste, your mind.
 
 Someone dating a singer is not “above” you. They just have a different life path and social circle.
 
 ---
 
 ### 5. The loneliness underneath
 
 What you wrote carries a few layers:
 - “Everyone has someone special; I don’t.”
 - “People might see me as less because of that.”
 - “Maybe I *am* less.”
 
 That last one is the dangerous lie.
 
 Being single:
 - Does not mean you’re unwanted.
 - Does not mean you’re behind.
 - Does not mean you’re less attractive or less worthy.
 
 It means:
 - You haven’t met the right person *in the right conditions* yet.
 - Or you’ve been busy surviving other things.
 - Or your standards/needs don’t match the people around you right now.
 
 None of that is a moral failure.
 
 ---
 
 ### 6. If you *do* want to meet someone (famous or not)
 
 If your real wish is:
 - “I want to be loved and chosen,” 
 that’s completely human.
 
 If your wish is:
 - “I want to date someone impressive so people stop looking down on me,” 
 that’s understandable too—but that path usually leads to more emptiness, not less.

 - Focus on meeting people who:
  - Respect you,
  - Make you feel safe,
  - Don’t treat you like a prop.

 - Hobbies, classes, communities,

 - Moving closer to those industries (media, events, entertainment, etc.),
 - Or building a life that naturally overlaps with those spaces.
 
 But that’s a *life choice*, not a requirement for worth.
 
 ---

 - **Name what’s actually hurting**

  - “I feel lonely” 
  - “I feel inferior” 
  - “I feel invisible” 
  - “I feel like I’m falling behind” 
  Knowing which one it is helps us respond to it.
 
 - **Limit exposure to people who flex status** 
  You don’t have to cut them off completely, but you can:
  - Share less of your vulnerable side with them.
  - Spend more time with people who don’t measure worth by status.

  When your brain says: 
  “They’re dating an announcer, I’m nothing,”

  “They’re dating an announcer. I’m still building my life. That doesn’t make me less.”

 - A way to respond (out loud or just in your head),
 - And some concrete steps to make your own life feel more like *yours*, not a comparison chart."

 I’ll answer both parts:
 1. The practical side: how people actually meet “famous” people 
 2. The emotional side: why this feels so painful and what you can do about it
 
 ---
 
 ## 1. How do people meet famous / high‑status partners?
 
 Most of the time, it’s not magic. It’s usually one of these:
 
 - **Work / industry overlap** 
  People in media, entertainment, events, PR, fashion, nightlife, etc. naturally cross paths with announcers, singers, influencers, etc. 
  Example: staff at TV stations, radio, production companies, agencies, event organizers, stylists, photographers.
 
 - **Social circles & introductions** 
  Friends of friends, private gatherings, after-parties, industry dinners. Once someone is in that circle, they keep meeting similar people.
 
 - **Shared hobbies / spaces** 
  - Gyms, dance studios, music studios, language schools, acting classes, etc. 
  - Charity events, fan clubs that are more “inner circle,” VIP sections at clubs.
 
 - **Online (but with filters)** 
  Some public figures use dating apps under different names or more private apps, or they meet people through Instagram DMs, etc.—but usually still through some kind of network or mutuals.
 
 So if your job and social life are outside those worlds, it’s completely normal that you’re not randomly dating announcers and singers. That doesn’t mean you’re less attractive or less worthy; it just means you’re in a different ecosystem.
 
 If you *really* wanted to increase the chance of meeting that type of person, it would look like:
 - Being around those industries (work, side jobs, volunteering at events, etc.)
 - Being active in hobbies/spaces where those people actually go

 ### 1. “Everyone around me is dating an announcer or a singer”

 - Work in related industries (media, entertainment, events, PR, etc.)

 - Working in those fields, or
 - Actively orbiting those social spaces,

 - Move closer to those industries (work, volunteering, side gigs, events).
 - Build your own skills and presence so you’re there as a peer, not as someone trying to “get” a famous partner.

 - To feel chosen,
 - To feel respected,
 - To not be the “only one without anyone”?

 This is the part that hurts the most.

 - If your “value” in that group depends on who you’re dating, you’re not being seen as a full person—just as an accessory.
 
 You deserve:
 - To be respected whether you’re single or not.
 - To be seen for your character, kindness, talents, and inner life—not your partner’s job title.

 - **How they treat you**, and
 - **What you start to believe about yourself because of that.**
 
 Their behavior might be real and shitty. 
 But the conclusion “I’m less than them” is *not* a fact; it’s a wound.
 
 ---
 
 ### 4. You’re not “behind” or “less than” for being single at 30
 
 Being 30 and single is:
 - Extremely common.
 - Not a moral failure.
 - Not a sign that you’re unlovable.
 
 What *is* painful is:
 - Watching others pair up while you feel left out.
 - Feeling like you have no one who chooses you.
 - Being in a social environment that equates love with status.

 - Your circle might be unusually connected to media/entertainment.

 - You’re less valuable because you’re single.

 - To not have your value measured by who you’re dating.

 - Want a partner you feel proud of.
 - Want to feel chosen, special, admired.

 - Do I want **their status**, or do I want a **real connection**?

 - You can date someone famous and still feel lonely, insecure, or looked down on.
 - You can date someone not-famous and feel deeply cherished and safe.
 
 You’re allowed to want both excitement and stability—but your worth doesn’t rise or fall with how impressive your partner looks to others.

  - Some are unstable.

 - Emotional availability
 - Stability

  - Doctors, therapists, trainers, coaches, teachers who have celebrity clients.

 5. **Status circles**
  - People from wealthy or influential families.
  - People who already have some level of public profile themselves (influencers, models, etc.).
 
 6. **Occasionally: pure chance**
  - Same café, same gym, same event, same flight.

 ---
 
 ### 3. The deeper pain: feeling like “the only one without anyone”
 
 This part matters more than the “famous” part.
 
 You wrote: “I’m the only one without anyone.” That sounds lonely and heavy.
 
 Underneath that, I hear things like:
 - “Am I unlovable?”
 - “Am I being left behind?”
 - “Is there something wrong with me?”
 
 Those are brutal questions to carry around. But they’re also **not accurate reflections of reality**; they’re reflections of how much you’re hurting.
 
 Being single right now means:
 - You haven’t met the right person yet 
 - Or you’re in a phase of life where other things are taking priority 
 - Or you’ve been hurt before and are more cautious
 
 It does **not** mean:
 - You’re less attractive
 - You’re less worthy
 - You’re someone people can look down on
 
 ---
 
 ### 4. About the people who look down on you
 
 If your friends or people around you:
 - Brag about their partners’ status
 - Make comments that imply you’re “less” because you’re single or not dating someone impressive
 - Treat relationships like trophies
 
 Then:
 - Their value system is shallow 
 - Their behavior is unkind 
 - You are allowed to feel hurt and also allowed to **step back** from that energy
 
 You deserve people who:
 - Care about how you’re doing, not who you’re dating 
 - Don’t measure your worth by your relationship status 
 - Don’t make you feel like you’re “behind” for living at your own pace
 
 ---
 
 ### 5. If you *do* want to meet more people (famous or not)
 
 If what you truly want is connection (not just status), then the question becomes: 
 “How can I build a life where I meet people who match me?”
 
 Some options, depending on your situation:
 
 - **If you want to meet more “high-profile” people:**
  - Get involved in **industries around them**: events, media, music, broadcasting, production, PR, fan communities that lead to staff/volunteer roles.
  - Go to **events** where they appear: signings, talks, festivals, live shows, networking events.
  - But: going in with the mindset “I must date someone famous” will usually feel bad and forced. Going in with “I want to be around things I genuinely enjoy” is healthier.
 
 - **If what you really want is just a kind, loving partner:**
  - Focus on **places where your values and interests are shared**: classes, hobbies, online communities, apps (used carefully), volunteer work.
  - Work on building a life that feels meaningful to you, even without a partner. That energy is attractive and also protects you from clinging to the wrong person just to not be alone.
 
 ---
 
 ### 6. You’re not an object to be ranked
 
 It sounds like the environment around you is very status-focused:
 - Age (“You’re 30, shouldn’t you rest?”)
 - Partner’s fame
 - Maybe looks, money, career too
 
 In that kind of environment, it’s easy to start seeing yourself like:
 - A product with a “value” that’s going down
 - Someone who has to “prove” they’re not a loser
 
 You are not a product. You’re a person. Your worth is **not negotiable** and **not dependent** on:
 - Who you date
 - How old you are
 - What other people think is impressive
 
 ---
 
 If you’re willing, tell me:
 
 1. Are these people who look down on you close friends, coworkers, family, or more like acquaintances? 
 2. What do *you* actually want right now: a relationship in general, a high-status partner, or mostly just not to feel so alone and judged?
 
 I can help you with:
 - Ways to protect yourself emotionally in this kind of social environment 
 - How to respond (or not respond) when people make these comments 
 - Concrete steps to build connection that feels real, not just impressive on the outside"
